64 CHAPTER 2 'escribe Azure architecture and services
.ubernetes creates containers in a pod . A pod is a group of related containers, and con -
tainers within a pod can share resources. 7his is one of the advantages to using .ubernetes 
because it releases you from the resourcesharing restriction typically imposed in a multi
container environment. However, a container in one pod is not able to share resources with 
a container in another pod.
7he computer that .ubernetes pods are running on is called a node or a worker. 7his com-
puter must have a container runtime such as 'ocNer running on it. ,n addition to pods, the 
node also runs several services that are reTuired for .ubernetes to manage the pods and so on. 
7here will typically be multiple nodes within a .ubernetes instance, and they are all controlled 
by a node called the .ubernetes control plane . 7he entire environment of the control plane and 
all its nodes is called a .ubernetes cluster .
A .ubernetes control plane contains all the configuration and services necessary to manage 
the orchestration of pods and other .ubernetes entities. &onfiguring a cluster can be complex, 
and it is by far the most laborious tasN of using .ubernetes. )or that reason, services such as 
Azure .ubernetes 6ervice A.6 are becoming more popular.
A.6 ofÁoads the burden of dealing with the .ubernetes cluster to Microsoft. :hen you 
create a .ubernetes cluster in A.6, Azure creates the control plane and the nodes for you. 
All you have to do is deploy your containers and you·re up and running with a managed 
.ubernetes cluster.
A.6 simplifies the creation of a .ubernetes cluster, but it also maNes it extremely easy to 
manage a cluster. 2perations such as upgrading a cluster or scaling a cluster are simple using 
the Azure portal menu options. <ou can also get detailed information on your cluster, includ-
ing each node that·s running in the cluster.
:hile A.6 maNes adopting and managing .ubernetes easier, it doesn·t completely obfus-
cate .ubernetes. 7o deploy your applications, you still need to understand how to use .uber -
netes, and in some cases, you·ll need to use the .ubernetes command line. Azure, however, 
maNes it far easier than doing all the legworN and maintenance yourself. Even better, A.6 in 
Azure is free. <ou only pay for the Azure compute resources you use within your cluster.

Virtual machines
7he application hosting options mentioned up to this point are all 3aa6 services. 7hey maNe 
application hosting easier because they remove your responsibility for creating and managing 
the infrastructure necessary for application hosting. However, if you want maximum control

:hen using virtual machines for application hosting, you·ll need to ensure you create the 
resources and configure everything for maximum security, and all of that is left to you. )or ex-

liNely want to install a firewall of some sort, possibly a load balancer to distribute load across 
multiple 9Ms, and so forth.

internet to the Azure resource.

Azure DNS
Azure '16 is a service for managing your '16 'omain 1ame 6ystem records with the added 
benefit of being integrated with Azure. 7o understand what that means, let·s brieÁy discuss 
'16 in general.
As you may already Nnow, computers on a networN communicate with each other using an 
,3 address. ,3 addresses can be i3v addresses such as 
192.168.0.4 or i3v6 addresses such as 
ff80::d432:9186:3d17:daee%5. ,n many scenarios, what·s efficient and simple for a computer 
isn·t Tuite so simple for humans, and ,3 addresses are a great example of that. -ust imagine if 
you had to Nnow the ,3 address for every website you wanted to visit. :orse still, an ,3 address 
might change, and unless you were aware of such a change, you might not be able to access 
that website.
7he '16 system solves this problem for us by mapping a name to the ,3 address. A full 
discussion of '16 is out of scope for this booN, but in the most basic sense, when you enter a 
domain name such as www.microsoft.com  into your browser, the '16 system is responsible for 
mapping that name to the ,3 address needed to reach the Microsoft website.
,f you want to purchase your own domain name, you go to a domain registrar, and they 
register the domain to you. 7hey also give you access to manage your '16 settings. 7hose set -
tings consist of nameserver settings and '16 records. 7he nameserver setting is the ,3 address 
typically more than one of the '16 server that Nnows the ,3 address of your domain name. 
7he '16 records are used to inform those nameservers how to reach your domain.
7here are numerous '16 records. )or example, an A address record maps your domain 
name to the i3v address of the computer hosting your domain. An AAAA record does the 
same for an i3v6 address. A &1AME canonical name record maps a name to another name or 
to an ,3 address. An M; mail exchanger record is used to map to a mail server for the domain.
MORE INFO HOSTNAMES AND SUBDOMAINS
An A record or AAAA record maps a hostname e.g., microsoft.com to an IP address. 
A CNAME maps a subdomain or domain alias e.g., www.microsoft.com  to a hostname. 
Without a CNAME record pointing www.microsoft.com  to the microsoft.com hostname, you 
would not be able to use www.microsoft.com  in a browser to reach Microsoft’s website.
,t·s perfectly fine to manage your '16 records at your domain registrar, but what if you could 
manage them inside the Azure portal instead" <ou·d then have access to all the tools Azure pro-
vides such as access control to allow others to manage your '16 records, logging to Neep an eye 
on changes, policies to apply governance, and so forth. 7hat·s what Azure '16 provides.
7o use Azure '16, you start by creating a '16 zone. 7here are two types of '16 zones in 
Azure '16.

/uni25A0Private zone =one used for '16 entries for use by an Azure virtual networN.

EXAM TIP
In any networking discussion, you may see references to public and private endpoints. A pub-
lic endpoint has an IP address that is accessible over the internet. A private endpoint has an IP 
address that is only accessible over a private network. It’s important to understand that even 
if a resource has a private endpoint, it may also have a public endpoint. For example, an Azure 
VM has a private IP address private endpoint that isn’t accessible from the internet, but it 
may also have a public IP address public endpoint that is accessible from the internet.
A public DNS zone contains entries for a public endpoint, and a private DNS zone contains 
entries for a private endpoint.
7o use a public '16 zone, create a '16 =one resource in the Azure portal, as shown in 
)igure 228. 7he name of your '16 zone should be the domain name that you are going to 
use with the '16 zone.

subnet that is created explicitly for the 931 *ateway. 7hese 9Ms run services that implement 
the functionality of 931 *ateway, and they also have networN routing tables that enable them

Azure Blob storage
Azure Blob storage is designed for storing data with no defined structure. 7hat includes text 
files, images, videos, documents, and much more. An entity stored in Blob storage is referred 
to as a Blob. 7here are three types of Blobs.
/uni25A0Block Blobs 7hese are used to store files used by an application.
/uni25A0Append Blobs 7hese are liNe BlocN Blobs, but Append Blobs are specialized for ap-
pend operations. )or that reason, they are often used to store constantly updated data 
liNe diagnostic logs.
/uni25A0Page Blobs 7hese are used to store virtual hard disN .vhd files that are used in Azure 
virtual machines. 
Blobs are stored in storage containers . A container is used as a means of organizing Blobs, 
so you might have a container for video files, another container for image files, and so on. 7he 
choice, however, is entirely up to you.
'ata that you store in Blob storage is stored in a specific storage tier. 7he reTuirements and 
cost of storage differ depending on the tier. :e·ll discuss this in more detail in the ´6torage 
tiersµ section later in this chapter.
Blob storage is a great option when you need an application to have easy and reliable 
access to binary data. )or example, you might use Blob storage to store video files that your 
application needs to stream, or you might use it to store large log files used in your application.
Azure Disks
Azure 'isNs refers to disNs that are used in virtual machines. Azure creates a disN that is auto-
matically designated for temporary storage when you create a 9M. 7his means data on that 
disN will be lost if there·s a maintenance event on the 9M. ,f you need to store data for a longer 
period that will persist between 9M deployments and maintenance events, you can create a 
data disN that is stored in Azure 'isNs.
Azure 'isNs are available as both traditional hard disNs H'' and solidstate drives 66'. 
H'' disNs are cheaper and designed for noncritical data. 66' disNs are available in a 6tandard 
tier for light use and as Azure 3remium 'isN for heavy use.
:hen you create a new disN, you can choose to create it from a snapshot of another disN, a 
Blob in Blob storage, or you can create an empty disN. A snapshot is a full copy of another disN. 
<ou can create a snapshot of a disN by creating a new 6napshot resource in the Azure portal 
and selecting the disN you want a snapshot of.
EXAM TIP
You can create a snapshot of a VM’s OS disk or a data disk. If you create a snapshot of a VM’s 
OS disk, you can use that snapshot to create another VM or to simply create a backup of the 
VM at a specific point in time.

MORE INFO INTERACTING WITH AZURE QUEUES
Message queues aren’t created and managed using the Azure portal. Instead, queues and 
messages are typically managed programmatically within an application. Microsoft offers 
support for most programming languages, including .NET, -ava, Python, Node.Ms, C, P+P, 
and Ruby.
You can also use The Az PowerShell module to create and manage queues and messages.
Each Tueue is given a lowercase name, and the 8R/ for accessing the Tueue uses that name. 
)or example, if we use a Tueue name of videos-to-encode and our storage account name is 
az900storage, the 8R/ to the message Tueue would be https://az900storage.queue.core.win-
dows.net/videos-to-encode . 
EXAM TIP
A message has a default time-to-live of seven days. +owever, when you add a message to a 
queue, you can specify a time-to-live of your choosing. Once the time-to-live expires, the 
message is deleted.
Storage tiers
Microsoft offers numerous storage tiers for Blob 6torage that are priced according to how of -
ten the data is accessed, how long you intend to store the data, and so on. 7he Hot storage tier 
is for data you need to access often. ,t has the highest cost of storage, but the cost of accessing 
the data is low. 7he &ool storage tier is for data that you intend to store for a longer period and 
not access Tuite as often. ,t has a lower storage cost than the Hot tier, but the access costs are 
higher. <ou·re also reTuired to Neep data in storage for at least 30 days.
Microsoft also offers an Archive storage tier for longterm data storage. 'ata stored in the 
Archive tier enMoys the lowest storage costs available, but the access costs are the highest. <ou 
must Neep data in storage for a minimum of 180 days in the Archive tier or you can be sub-
Mected to an early deletion charge. Because data in the Archive tier isn·t designed for TuicN and 
freTuent access, it can taNe a very long time to retrieve it. ,n fact, while the Hot and &ool access 
tiers guarantee access to the first byte of data within milliseconds, the Archive tier only guaran -
tees access to the first byte within 15 hours unless you use the high priority option.
EXAM TIP
If you need to access a Blob in the Archive tier, you must first move it into the +ot or Cool 
tier. The process of moving the Blob to the +ot or Cool tier is called rehydration .

'ata in Azure 6torage can be replicated in the primary region the region you select when 
you create the storage account or across regions depending on which redundancy option 
you choose.
EXAM TIP
You cannot change the redundancy type of an Azure storage account after the storage ac -
count has been created.
Primary-region redundancy
7here are two redundancy options designed to replicate your data only in the primary region. 
7hey are locally redundant storage /R6 and zoneredundant storage =R6. :hen using /R6, 
Azure maNes three copies of your data within a single datacenter. =R6 also maNes three copies 
of your data, but each copy is in a separate availability zone in a separate datacenter.
MORE INFO AVAILABILITY ZONES
Not all Azure regions include support for availability zones. Those that don’t will also not 
have support for ZRS. For more information on availability zones, see “Availability zonesµ in 
Skill ., “Describe the core architectural components of Azure.µ
/R6 is the least expensive redundancy option, but it·s also the least durable. Microsoft rec-
ommends you use /R6 only if you can easily recreate any data that is lost. 7his is because /R6 
only protects your data from a problem with a drive or a server racN where your data resides.
:hen you use =R6, each copy of your data is in a separate datacenter because Azure uses 
availability zones for redundancy. =R6 has the added benefit of protecting your data from a 
problem impacting a single datacenter, but it won·t protect you from a largescale disaster in 
an Azure region.
/R6 or =R6 are freTuently chosen by companies that are reTuired to Neep all data within 
the boundaries of a country. :hen you move to data replicated across multiple regions, you 
may end up in a scenario where your data crosses geographical boundaries, and if regulations 
forbid that, /R6 or =R6 may be your only viable option.
MORE INFO WRITES ARE SYNCHRONOUS
Data writes to Azure Storage in LRS and ZRS are synchronous. That means that a write to 
Azure Storage is only successful once the data is successfully written to all three copies.
MORE INFO

have support for ZRS. For more information on availability zones, see “Availability zonesµ in 
Skill ., “Describe the core architectural components of Azure.µ

Multiple-region redundancy
Azure can also distribute copies of your data across Azure regions. 7hese options protect you 
from a largescale impact in a particular Azure region. -ust as with primaryregion redundancy, 
there are two redundancy options for multipleregion redundancy. 7hey are georedundant 
storage *R6 and geozoneredundant storage *=R6.
*R6 creates three copies of your data in the primary region using /R6. ,t then creates an ad-
ditional three copies of your data using /R6 in a separate Azure region that·s hundreds of miles 
away from the primary region.
*=R6 creates three copies of your data in three availability zones in the primary region 
using =R6. ,t then creates three copies of your data using /R6 in a secondary region that is hun-
dreds of miles away from the primary region.
EXAM TIP
When using *RS or *ZRS, data in the primary region is replicated synchronously, but data 
is replicated to the secondary region asynchronously. Therefore, there is a small delay in 
synchronizing the data between the primary and secondary regions.
<ou might wonder why *=R6 uses /R6 for the redundant data in the secondary region. 7he 
reason is that the replicated data in the secondary region is there for bacNup purposes only by 
default. Access to the data in the secondary region is available only if there is a failure in the 
primary region. ,n that case, Azure will change the '16 information to your storage account so 
that any access reTuests are sent to the secondary region.
,f you reTuire it, you can enable read access to the data in the secondary region by 
checNing the ´MaNe read access to data available in the event of regional unavailabilityµ 
checNbox shown previously in )igure 236. :hen using this option, the two multipleregion 
redundancy options are referred to as read access georedundant storage RA*R6 and 
read access geozoneredundant storage RA*=R6. 7his option is available for all storage 
services except Azure )iles.
MORE INFO USING READ ACCESS REDUNDANCY OPTIONS
Earlier in this chapter, I said Azure will update the DNS records for your storage account in a 
failover situation where access to data is required in the secondary region. If you use one of 
the read access options, Azure creates a URL for accessing the secondary region by append -
ing 
-secondary to the storage account name in the URL.

Directory services in Azure
,n most business applications, not all users have the same privileges. )or example, a website 
might allow a small number of users to add and revise content. Another smaller group of 
users might be able to decide who can add content. Most users, however, are Must consumers 
of the content. 7hey can·t modify the content in any way, and they also can·t grant other 
people the ability to access the content.
7o implement this Nind of control, you need to Nnow who is using the application so you can 
determine what their level of privileges should be. 7o determine who is using the application, 
you would reTuire that users log in, often with a username and password. Assuming the user 
provides the right credentials, that user is authenticated to the application.
2nce a user is authenticated and begins interacting with an application, additional checNs 
might taNe place to confirm which actions the user is and isn·t allowed to perform. 7hat process 
is called authorization, and authorization checNs are performed against a user who is al-
ready authenticated.
7his Nind of authentication and authorization scenario isn·t limited to a website scenario. 
:hen you log into the Azure portal, you are being authenticated. As you interact with Azure 
resources, you are also authorized to perform the actions you·re taNing. Based on your privi-
lege level, you are only allowed to do certain things. )or example, you might be authorized to 
create Azure resources but not authorized to give other people access to the Azure subscrip -
tion you·re using.
Azure uses a service called Azure Active 'irectory to enforce authentication and authoriza -
tion in Azure, but it has many capabilities beyond simply authentication and authorization.
Azure Active Directory
Azure Active 'irectory Azure A' is an identity service that can control access to resources based 
on an identity. Azure A' helps you authenticate and authorize users. <ou can use Azure A' to give 
users access to Azure resources. <ou can also give users access to thirdparty resources used by 
your company and to onpremises resources, all using the same username and password.

,n the simplest sense, administrators of Azure A' can decide if a user has access to a particu-

has the authorization to access that resource. However, most administrators want much more 
control than that to Neep resources secure.
6uppose you have given a user named &hristine access to your Azure resources, and you

data. Azure &onditional Access and multifactor authentication M)A can help maNe &hristine·s 
account much more secure, and single sign-on can help Christine access her corporate resourc -
es without having to enter her username and password. /et·s start by looNing at single signon.

6ingle signon 662 is a simple concept with a huge impact for users. :hen using 662, users 
can access corporate resources at their companies without having to enter a username and 
password. ,nstead, they are authenticated using the login credentials they supplied when log-

)or a device to worN with 662, it must be Moined to Azure A'. 2nce the device has been added

98 CHAPTER 2 'escribe Azure architecture and services
EXAM TIP
SSO to on-premises resources is implemented using Azure AD Connect.
662 supports two signin methods password hash synchronization and passthrough 
authentication. 3assword hash synchronization copies a user·s password in a hashed format to 
Azure A'. Because the password is hashed, the actual password can·t be retrieved. :hen the 
user enters the password, an algorithm is used to generate a hash, and that hash is compared 
against the hash stored in Azure A'. ,f the two are the same, the user is authenticated.
3assthrough authentication passes a user·s login on Azure A' into an onpremises pass
through authentication agent, and that agent sends the authentication to the on-premises 
:indows Active 'irectory instance. 2nce authenticated, Azure A' &onnect is used to pass that 
authentication through to Azure A' and the user·s resources.
Multifactor authentication MFA
By default, users can log in to your Azure A' using only a username and password. Even if 
you reTuire your users to use strong passwords, allowing access to your resources with only 
a username and password is risNy. ,f a hacNer obtains the password by using software that 
guesses passwords or by stealing it through phishing or some other means, your resources are 
no longer secure.
Multifactor authentication solves this problem. 7he concept behind multifactor authentica -
tion is that you must authenticate using a combination of
/uni25A06omething you Nnow, such as a username and password
/uni25A06omething you have, such as a phone or mobile device
/uni25A06omething you are, such as facial recognition or a fingerprint
,f multifactor authentication reTuires all three of these, it·s referred to as threefactor 
authentication, or sometimes 3)A. ,f only the first two are reTuired, it·s referred to as two
factor authentication, or sometimes 2)A. Microsoft calls this two-step verification. Azure 
multifactor authentication is twofactor authentication.
NOTE BIOMETRICS IN MOBILE DEVICES
Even though Azure multifactor authentication is two-factor, if you are using a device that 
includes biometric features, you might be authenticating using three-factor authentication. 
+owever, the third factor is enforced by your device and not by Azure. Azure multifactor 
authentication doesn’t require three-factor authentication.
NOTE

Passwordless authentication

people find multifactor authentication to be a hassle. 3asswordless authentication provides the 
same level of security while removing the hassle of multifactor authentication.

authenticating users with something they know a password and something they have, 
passwordless can authenticate users with something they have a device or security key and 
something they are via biometrics.
+aving said that, passwordless can also authenticate using something a user knows when it 
allows for authentication using a PIN.

Role-based access control RBAC
Rolebased access control RBA& is a generic term that refers to the concept of authorizing 
users to a system that is based on defined roles to which the user belongs. Azure implements 
RBA& across all Azure resources, so you can control how users and applications can interact 
with your Azure resources.
<ou might want to allow users who administer your databases to have access to databases in 
a particular resource group, but you don·t want to allow those people to create new databases 
or delete existing databases. <ou might also want some web developers to be able to deploy 
new code to your web applications, but you don·t want them to be able to scale the app to a 
higherpriced plan. 7hese are Must two examples of what you can do with RBA& in Azure.
7here are four elements to RBA&
/uni25A0Security principal 7he security principal represents an identity. ,t can be a user, a 
group, an application which is called a service principal, or a special AA' entity called 
a managed identity. A managed identity is how you authorize another Azure service to 
access your Azure resource.
/uni25A0Role A role sometimes called a role definition is what defines how the security 
principal can interact with an Azure resource. )or example, a role might define that a 
security principal can read the properties of a resource but cannot create new resources 
or delete existing resources.
/uni25A0Scope 7he scope defines the level at which the role is applied, and it specifies how 
much control the security principal has. )or example, if the scope is a resource group, 
the role defines activities that can be performed on all resources in the resource group.
/uni25A0Role assignments Roles are assigned to a security principal at a particular scope, and 
that·s what ultimately defines the level of access for the security principal.
RBA& includes many builtin roles. 7hree of these builtin roles apply to all Azure resources
/uni25A0Owner Members of this role have full access to the resources.
/uni25A0Contributor Members of this role can create resources and manage resources, but 
they cannot delegate that right to anyone else.
/uni25A0Reader Members of this role can see Azure resources, but they cannot create, delete, 
or manage those resources.
All the other builtin roles are specific to certain types of Azure resources.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

EXAM TIP
The scope of RBAC is defined by where the RBAC role is assigned. For example, if you open 
a resource group in the portal and assign an RBAC role to a user, the scope is at the resource 
group level. On the other hand, if you open a web app within that resource group and assign 
the role, the scope is to that web app only.
RBAC roles can be scoped to the management group, subscription, resource group, or 
resource level.

EXAM TIP
It’s important to understand that role assignments are additive. Your RBAC abilities at any 
scope are the result of all role assignments up to that level. In other words, if I have the Own -
er role on a resource group and you assign me the Website Contributor role on a web app 
within that resource group, the Website Contributor assignment will have no effect because 
I already have the Owner role on the entire resource group.

Defense in depth and Zero-trust
-ust for a moment, transport yourself bacN to medieval times and thinN about what it was liNe 
living in a castle. 7hese weren·t friendly times in many ways, and there was always a hostile 
force trying to enter the fortress. 7o prevent invasion, moats were built around castles. 7he 
purpose of the moat was to prevent an opposing force from tunneling under the wall and 
gaining entry.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

114 CHAPTER 2 'escribe Azure architecture and services
Even before an enemy reached the moat, archers along the high wall of the castle would 
pose a formidable risN to attacNers approaching the castle. Assuming an opposing force made 
it past the archers and traversed the moat, they were met with a high wall and a sturdy gate. 
,f they were able to maNe it past the gate, they were met with an army of warriors with swords 
and other nasty weapons.
Medieval folNs had a pretty good idea when it came to security. 7hey realized that a single 
opposing force wouldn·t be enough to Neep them secure. 7hey needed layered opposition so 
that anyone defeating one method of security would be met with several more down the line.
7his is a perfect example of defense in depth, and it·s why defense in depth is often referred 
to as the ´castle approach.µ :hen it comes to networN security, this multilayered approach is 
also the best way to Neep your networN safe. Azure )irewall can help prevent a malicious user 
from maNing it into your networN, 1etworN 6ecurity *roups can help you control networN traf-
fic inside your networN, and Azure ''o6 3rotection can help to identify and mitigate malicious 
traffic that might otherwise seem normal.
7he concept of defense in depth is only a starting point when it comes to securing your 
resources. 7o implement and maintain a solid security approach, you must have a strategy in 
place. :hile that sounds reasonable, many of us are not security experts. 7hanNfully, Microsoft 
has developed a strategy that you can adopt called =erotrust.
A traditional approach to security was to consider everything happening inside of an 
internal networN as trusted. :hile operations on resources in that networN relied on proper 
authorization, traffic within the networN was not considered a breach of security. 
7his model worNed well for a long time, but as the ,7 world has evolved, it has become too 
restrictive. 7his has been made especially clear due to the enormous increase of the hybrid 
worNforce in the era of &ovid19.
MORE INFO IMPLEMENTING ZERO-TRUST
Microsoft has a documented plan for implementing Zero-trust that can help you transition. 
You can find it at https://bit.ly/az900-zerotrust .
7he =erotrust frameworN relies on identifying users, but instead of simply locNing down a 
networN, =erotrust uses &onditional Access policies to control access to resources. 7his model 
extends to networN endpoints, data, apps, infrastructure, and the networN. Multifactor authen-
tication is also a cornerstone of this approach.
=erotrust doesn·t end with authentication. <our apps and your networNs must also adopt 
=erotrust principles. Apps should be designed to allow the lowest level of access necessary, 
and every access to an app and its data should be assumed to be a breach until determined 
otherwise.
1etworNs should taNe the same approach by using features such as Azure )irewall, 1etworN 
6ecurity *roups, ''o6 protections, Azure 931 *ateway, and so forth.

Chapter summary
7he scope of this chapter was enormous :e started with the basics of Azure architecture and 
ended up discussing a wide array of Azure services. :e covered concepts related to identity, 
security, and more.
Here·s a summary of everything this chapter covered
/uni25A0Azure is organized by geographies and regions.
/uni25A0Regions have a region pair for high availability.
/uni25A06overeign regions account for the reTuirements of governments and countries.
/uni25A0Availability zones ensure high availability if a problem is encountered in a datacenter.
/uni25A0An enabled region has at least three availability zones.
/uni25A0An Azure datacenter is a physical building in a region with its own power, water, cooling, 
and networN system.
/uni25A0A resource group is a logical entity for organizing Azure resources.
/uni25A0An Azure subscription is used to give you access to create and manage Azure resources.
/uni25A0A management group is a logical entity for managing subscriptions. A management 
group can only contain subscriptions and other management groups.
/uni25A0Azure &ontainer ,nstances A&, is a serverless option for easily running containerized 
worNloads.
/uni25A0Azure )unctions is a service for hosting microservices in the cloud.
/uni25A0Azure offers the ability to create 9Ms easily with a choice of multiple operating systems.
/uni25A0Availability sets provide a virtual representation of a networN racN a fault domain and 
an update domain. 7he fault domain protects against a fault in the racN, and the update 
domain ensures availability during reboots from updates.
/uni25A09irtual machine scale sets 9M66 allow you to scale 9Ms easily.
/uni25A0Azure 9irtual 'esNtop A9' provides desNtop virtualization for running operating 
systems and apps using a client for :indows, mac26, i26, or Android. ,t can also be ac -
cessed via a web browser.
/uni25A0&reating a 9M in Azure creates many additional resources under the hood, including 
networN interfaces, virtual networNs, and disNs.
/uni25A0Azure App 6ervice is a 3aa6 service for easily hosting web apps in the cloud.
/uni25A0App 6ervice runs web apps in App 6ervice plans.
/uni25A0Azure .ubernetes 6ervice A.6 is a 3aa6 offering for hosting .ubernetes clusters run -
ning containerized applications.
/uni25A0Azure 6pring &loud maNes hosting 6pring apps in the cloud easy.
/uni25A0Azure virtual networNs 91ets are used to create networN environments in the cloud.

Chapter summary CHAPTER 2 125
/uni25A091et peering can be used to connect two 91ets to each other. *lobal 91et peering 
connects 91ets in separate regions.

CHAPTER 3

and governance
As you move to the cloud, it’s important to plan accordingly. You must consider several 
factors. Cost is certainly one factor, but how you plan your resource governance is also 
important, and in most situations, you’ll also need to deal with compliance with your 
corporate policies.
As you deploy your resources, you’ll need to understand the options available for manag-
ing and deploying your resources. You’ve had a chance to see the Azure portal already, and 
now it’s time to dig deeper, as well as look at some of the available command-line options.
You’ll also need to understand how to monitor your resources, and this chapter will cover 
the tools that make that possible.
Skills covered in this chapter:
/uni25A0Describe cost management in Azure
/uni25A0Describe features and tools in Azure for governance and compliance
/uni25A0Describe features and tools for managing and deploying Azure resources
/uni25A0Describe monitoring tools in Azure
Skill 3.1: Describe cost management in Azure
Managing your costs in the cloud requires a different approach than when on-premises. In 
the cloud, there are factors that can dramatically impact your costs, and you’ll need to be 
aware of those factors. <ou may also need to accurately tracN the spending of specific areas 
of your organization or within specific deployments.
Azure provides information and tools that address all these needs. 
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

/uni25A0Factors that can affect costs 
/uni25A0Pricing calculator and Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) calculator
/uni25A0Azure Cost Management and Billing
/uni25A0Tags
Factors that can affect costs
As you’re planning your Azure deployments, you should keep in mind the factors that can af-
fect your costs, such as the resource type, how you purchase the resource, the Azure regions 
you use, and the billing zone your resources are in.
Azure services are billed according to meters  associated with a resource. These meters track 
how much a specific metric has been used by the resource. )or example, there is no charge 
specifically for an Azure virtual networN, and you aren·t charged for networN traffic within a 
virtual networN however, you are charged per gigabyte for traffic into and out of the virtual 
network from peered virtual networks.
EXAM TIP
Each Azure service has a pricing page that outlines estimates on pricing for that resource 
based on typical usage.
As you determine which resources you need to use in your Azure deployment, consider how 
those resources are going to use the metrics the resources charges for. )or example, if you can 
plan your virtual networks so that you have fewer peered networks, you can save substantially 
over the long term.
Also, there are ways to save money when purchasing Azure resources. )or example, Micro-
soft will offer you a reduced rate if you agree to pay in advance using an Enterprise Agreement. 
Longer-term agreements offer even more price breaks. Cloud Solution Providers (CSPs) might 
also provide you with complete solutions that are more cost-effective than purchasing all the 
resources yourself.
Microsoft’s costs for operating Azure services differ by region, even when those regions are 
within the same geographic boundary. Therefore, your pricing will differ based on which Azure 
region you use. )or example, a 9M deployed to the &entral 86 region will cost more than the 
same 9M deployed to the East 86 region. Microsoft doesn·t provide a breaNdown of its costs, 
but you can assume that electricity and other resources needed for an Azure data center are 
more expensive in the &entral 86 region than they are in the Eastern 86 region.

Skill 3.1: Describe cost management in Azure CHAPTER 3 129
EXAM TIP
Choosing the least-expensive region for each of your Azure resources usually isn’t a good 
way to control costs. You might have to pay for network traffic across regions, which could 
increase your costs above the amount you’re saving. Many Azure resources do not charge 
for network traffic within the same region, but they will charge for traffic across regions.
,t·s also important to Neep in mind that you·re not charged for networN traffic into an Azure 
datacenter, but you are charged for networN traffic out of a datacenter. However, your first 100*B 
of outbound data is free. After that point, you are charged a set amount on a tiered model.
MORE INFO PRICING OF NETWORK BANDWIDTH
For more information on pricing of network bandwidth in Azure, see 
https://bit.ly/az900-bandwidthpricing .
It’s also important to know that Azure regions are broken out into four separate groups for 
billing purposes. These groups are called billing zones , or more commonly, zones. Microsoft’s 
costs for networN traffic out of each zone differ, so your costs will differ, too.
Reducing Azure costs
It’s important to understand how your decisions can affect your costs, but it’s also important to 
understand those things you can do to reduce the costs you experience in Azure.
,f you regularly find that you use 9Ms month over month, you can save substantially by 
using Azure Reservations. By committing to resource usage in advance and taking advantage 
of reserved instances, you no longer must pay the fees associated with pay-as-you-go pricing. 
You can also modify your reserved instance reservation to adjust for your usage as you go, so 
you don·t have to sacrifice Áexibility.
Similarly, if you have a need for consistent usage of Azure SQL Database, Azure Cosmos DB, 
or Azure Synapse Analytics, you can save substantially with reserved capacity pricing on those 
services. Reserved capacity allows you to save by purchasing a one-year or three-year contract, 
and you are then billed monthly at a reduced amount.
<ou can save even more on 9Ms and Azure 64/ 'atabase by taNing advantage of hybrid 
use benefit. :hen you use Azure Hybrid Benefit, you bring your own license for :indows 
6erver or 64/ 6erver to Azure. )or example, if you use 10 :indows 9Ms for, say, a month, you 
can save more than 0 percent by using your own license with Azure Hybrid Benefit.
Another way to save money is by taking advantage of Microsoft’s unused server capacity to 
run workloads that only require the use of a computer for a temporary (usually short) timeframe. 
By using Azure 6pot 9Ms, you can taNe advantage of this unused capacity for hugesavings.
As you can see, many factors can affect your costs in Azure, and it can be difficult to esti-
mate costs based on all these factors. Fortunately, Microsoft offers calculators that can help 
you get a handle on estimating your costs as you move to the cloud.

Tags
7ags maNe tracNing expenses in Azure easy. A tag consists of a name and a value. )or example, 
suppose a company is participating in two trade events one in 7exas and one in 1ew <orN. <ou 
have also created a lot of Azure resources to support those events. You want to view all the 
Azure resources for a specific event, but they·re spread out across multiple resource groups. By 
adding a tag to each resource group that identifies the event it·s associated with, you can solve 
this problem.
Once you’ve added your tags, they will be visible on your Azure invoice, making them an 
ideal method of categorizing and reporting on costs.

Skill 3.2: Describe features and tools in Azure for 
governance and compliance
8nless you have some control over how your resources are created and managed, costs can 
spiral out of control. In addition to cost control, you might have other restrictions you’d like 
in place as well, such as which regions certain resources should be created in, how certain 
resources are tagged, and so on.
The traditional way of handling such governance issues would be to send out a memo 
to everyone explaining what the reTuirements are, and then crossing your fingers that 
people adhere to them. Fortunately, Azure Policy can ensure that your requirements and 
policies are adhered to.
Ensuring that you can re-create environments is another important aspect of governance, 
and Azure Blueprints is the perfect way to make sure you can re-create an environment 
with precision. 
Another important facet of the cloud is compliance. Companies moving to the cloud are 
often concerned about keeping information private and compliant with regulations. Microsoft 
addresses these concerns using many methods, and in this section, we’ll talk about how Azure 
can help with compliance.

142 CHAPTER 3 Describe Azure management and governance
Azure Blueprints
:hen a company decides to create a cloud application, it usually doesn·t start by creating a re-
source in the portal. Instead, a lot of planning happens before a single Azure resource is created. 
This includes a plan for making sure the entire architecture of the cloud app complies with the 
necessary standards. It also involves concepts like detailed planning of virtual network topologies 
and rights assignments for users of the app. Also, best practices are likely a part of this planning.
There’s a lot of risk involved if a company fails to plan carefully, and for that reason, many 
companies will hire someone with deep technical knowledge of the cloud to help in that plan-
ning. Hiring that Nind of resource can add a lot of additional expense, and it can also add a lot 
of time to a project.
Azure Blueprints is a service that can make the process of deploying to the cloud easier. 
Blueprints allow you to configure an environment Must as you need it to be, along with all the 
policies and other governance aspects in place. 7hat configuration can then be saved so it can 
be duplicated at any time in other deployments.
Items that you add to a blueprint are called artifacts. An artifact can be a resource group, 
an ARM template, a policy assignment, or a role assignment. Once you’ve created a blueprint, 
you can either save it in a subscription or management group. A blueprint that’s saved 
in a management group can then be used by any subscription within that management 
group’s hierarchy.
EXAM TIP
Because blueprints are actual Azure resources and not simply files designed to define a 
deployment, Azure maintains a connection between the blueprint and the resources that 
use the blueprint. That allows companies to iterate on blueprints and improve them. It also 
makes it much easier for a blueprint to evolve with a company’s needs. Also, blueprints 
are versioned and can be stored in a source-control system, so tracking blueprints is easy 
and effective.
With that said, it’s important to understand that blueprints aren’t a replacement for ARM 
templates. In fact, most blueprints make extensive use of ARM templates as artifacts. You’ll 
learn about ARM templates in Skill 3.3, “Describe features and tools for managing and de -
ploying Azure resources.”
Follow these steps to create a blueprint.
1. Search for blueprints  in the Azure portal to open the Blueprints _ *etting 6tarted page, 
as shown in Figure 3-14.

ployed with the resource being created or updated. If that resource type is not included, 
it is automatically deployed.
/uni25A0Disabled The policy is not in effect.

Resource locks
:hen you need to prevent changes to a resource, or prevent that resource from being deleted, 
resource locks (or locks) are a simple solution. Locks apply to everyone with access to the re-
source, regardless of which RBAC role they are assigned.
EXAM TIP
In order to create a lock, you must either be in the Owner or the User Access Administrator 
role in RBAC. Alternatively, an administrator can create a custom role that grants the right to 
create a lock.
Locks can be applied at the resource level, the resource group level, or at the subscription 
level. To apply a lock to a resource, open the resource in the Azure portal and click Locks  in the 
Settings section of the menu on the left, as shown in Figure 3-29.
FIGURE 3-29 Locking a resource
To add a lock to the resource, click Add. (You can also review and add locks to the resource 
group by clicking Resource Group , or to the subscription by clicking Subscription .) In the 
Lock Name box, provide a name for the locN set the Lock Type, and add an optional note, as 
shown in )igure 330.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

156 CHAPTER 3 Describe Azure management and governance
FIGURE 3-30 Adding a read-only lock
A read-only lock is the most restrictive lock. It prevents changing properties of the resource 
or deleting the resource. A delete lock prevents the resource from being deleted, but proper-
ties can still be changed. The result of a read-only lock is often unpredictable because of the 
way locks are handled by Azure.
Locks only apply to operations that are handled by Azure Resource Manager (ARM), and 
some operations specific to a resource are handled internally by the resource instead of being 
handled by ARM. )or example, if you set a readonly locN on an instance of Azure .ey 9ault, it 
will prevent a user from changing access policies on the vault, but users can still add and delete 
Neys, secrets, and certificates because those operations are handled internally by .ey 9ault.
MORE INFO AZURE RESOURCE MANAGER (ARM)
You’ll learn about ARM in Skill 3.3, “Describe features and tools for managing and deploying 
Azure resources.”
There are other situations where a read-only lock can prevent operations that occur un-
expectedly. )or example, if you place a readonly locN on a storage account, it will prevent all 
users from listing the access keys for the storage account because the operation to list keys 
makes the keys available for write access.
If a lock is applied to a resource group, all resources in that resource group inherit the lock. 
Similarly, if a lock is applied at the subscription level, all resources in the subscription inherit the 
lock. It is possible to nest locks, and in such situations, the most restrictive lock is the effec-
tive locN. )or example, if you have a readonly locN on a resource group and a delete locN on a 
resource in that resource group, the resource will have a read-only lock applied to it because a 
readonly locN is more restrictive. 7he explicit delete locN will be irrelevant.
MORE INFO
 AZURE RESOURCE MANAGER (ARM)
You’ll learn about ARM in Skill 3.3, “Describe features and tools for managing and deploying 
Azure resources.”
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Skill 3.2: Describe features and tools in Azure for governance and compliance CHAPTER 3 157
EXAM TIP
Locks are also inherited by newly created resources. If you apply a delete lock to a resource 
group and add a new resource to the resource group later, the new resource will automati-
cally inherit the delete lock.
:hen an operation is attempted in the portal and denied because of a locN, an error will 
display, as shown in Figure 3-31.
EXAM TIP
Not all resource types will tell you that a lock prevented an operation that was attempted 
in the portal. There are times when you will see only a generic error message. If you try 
the same operation in the Azure CLI or using the Az module in PowerShell, you should see 
details on the lock.
You can edit or delete a lock by clicking Locks  and clicking either Edit or Delete in the 
portal, as shown in Figure 3-32. In most cases, you will need to scroll to the right to see the Edit
and Delete buttons.
FIGURE 3-31 Denied by a lock

If you’re a PowerShell user, you can take advantage of that knowledge to manage your Azure

can run on Windows, Linux, or macOS.

Skill 3.3: Describe features and tools for managing and deploying Azure resources CHAPTER 3 169
Before you can use the PowerShell Az module, you·ll need to install it. 7o do that, you first 
need to run 3ower6hell elevated. ,n :indows, that means running it as an Administrator. ,n 
/inux and mac26, you·ll need to run it with superuser privileges using 6udo.
To install the module, run the following command.
Install-Module -Name Az -AllowClobber
:hen you install a new 3ower6hell module, 3ower6hell checNs all existing modules to see 
if they have any command names that are the same as a command name in the module you’re 
installing. If they do, the installation of the new module fails. By specifying 
-AllowClobber, you 
are telling PowerShell that it’s okay for the Az  module to take precedence for any commands 
that also exist in another module.
If you are unable to run PowerShell elevated, you can install the module for your user ID 
only by using the following command:
Install-Module -Name Az -AllowClobber -Scope CurrentUser
Once you’ve installed the module, you need to sign in with your Azure account. To do that, 
run the following command:
Connect-AzAccount
This command will display a token in the PowerShell window. You’ll need to browse to 
https://microsoft.com/devicelogin and enter the code to authenticate your PowerShell session. 
,f you close 3ower6hell, you·ll have to run the command again in your next session.
MORE INFO PERSISTING CREDENTIALS
It is possible to configure PowerShell to persist your credentials. For more information on 
doing that, see https://docs.microsoft.com/powershell/azure/context-persistence .
If you have more than one Azure subscription, you’ll want to set the active subscription so 
that commands you enter will affect the desired subscription. You can do that using the follow-
ing command:
Set-AzContext -Subscription “subscription_id”
Replace subscription_id with the subscription ID of your Azure subscription you want to 
use with the Az module.
All Az module commands will have a common syntax that starts with a verb and an obMect. 
9erbs are things liNe New, Get, Move, or Remove. The object is the thing that you want the verb 
to affect. )or example, the following command will create a resource group called MyRG in the 
6outh &entral 86 region
New-AzResourceGroup -Name MyRG -Location “South Central US”

178 CHAPTER 3 Describe Azure management and governance
Azure Arc
In Chapter 1, we discussed cloud models, and in that discussion, I pointed out that many com-
panies are adopting the hybrid cloud model. It’s common for companies to have some resourc-
es in Azure and some on-premises. It’s also not uncommon for people to have some resources 
in other cloud providers.
:hen resources are spread out in multiple environments, managing those resources can be 
difficult, especially when you want to apply some governance to those resources. 7o help with 
this problem, Microsoft released Azure Arc.
Azure Arc extends management and governance features of Azure to resources outside of 
Azure. It does this using one of two different methods: Azure Arc-enabled servers or Azure 
Arc–enabled .ubernetes.
Azure Arcenabled servers allow you to extend Azure management features to your physi-
cal servers and virtual machines running outside of Azure. To use Azure Arc-enabled servers, 
you will need to install the Azure Connected Machine agent to the machine. This agent collects 
information about the server or 9M and maNes it available to Azure. ,t also installs some exten-
sions from Azure onto your 9Ms that are used for 9M management.
EXAM TIP
Azure Arc-enabled servers can be Windows servers or Linux servers.
2nce the Azure &onnected Machine agent is installed, your server or 9M will have a man -
aged identity in Azure, allowing you to easily manage the server or 9M using Azure manage -
ment tools. This includes RBAC, Azure Policy, tags, protection with Azure Defender for Cloud, 
and more.
NOTE SQL SERVER ON ARC-ENABLED SERVERS
You can register your SQL Server instances running on Arc-enabled servers. Doing so makes 
it possible to manage those SQL Server instances using Azure.
Azure Arcenabled .ubernetes extends Azure management and governance features to 
your .ubernetes clusters running onpremises or in other cloud providers. /iNe Azure Arc
enabled servers, Azure Arcenabled .ubernetes worNs via the installation of an agent that you 
install. 2nce you·ve created your .ubernetes cluster outside of Azure, you can use the Azure 
CLI or PowerShell to begin the registration of your cluster with Azure Arc. 
:hen you register your cluster, Azure connects your .ubernetes cluster to Azure via a se -
cured connection, enabling you to manage the cluster using Azure features.
EXAM TIP
Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes uses a feature of the Git source control system called GitOps 
to deploy and manage Arc-enabled Kubernetes clusters.
NOTE
 SQL SERVER ON ARC-ENABLED SERVERS
You can register your SQL Server instances running on Arc-enabled servers. Doing so makes 
it possible to manage those SQL Server instances using Azure.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Arcenabled .ubernetes enables a lot of functionality besides simply managing .ubernetes 
clusters. Arc-enabled data services make it possible to run Azure SQL Managed Instance or 
3ostgre64/ Hyperscale services on your .ubernetes clusters outside of Azure. Azure appli-
cation services allow you to run apps on several Azure services on Arcenabled .ubernetes, 
including Azure App 6ervice, Azure )unctions, Azure /ogic Apps, Azure Event *rid, and Azure 
API Management.
7he functionality of Azure Arc has grown significantly since it was announced, and you can 
expect Microsoft to continue to leverage this technology to differentiate its offerings from 
competitors.
Azure Resource Manager (ARM) and ARM templates
Almost all systems that are moved to the cloud consist of more than one Azure service. )or ex-
ample, you might have an Azure virtual machine for one part of your app; your data might be 
in an Azure 64/ 'atabase you might have some sensitive data stored in Azure .ey 9ault and 
you might have a web-based portion of your app hosted in Azure App Service.
If you must manage all these different Azure services separately, it can be quite a headache, 
and if you have multiple applications in the cloud, it can be even worse. Not only would it be con-
fusing to keep track of which services are related to which applications, but when you add in the 
complexity of deploying updates to your application, things can really become disorganized.
To make it easier to deploy and manage Azure services, Microsoft developed Azure Re-
source Manager (ARM). ARM is a service that runs in Azure, and it’s responsible for all interac-
tions with Azure services. :hen you create a new Azure service, ARM authenticates you to 
ensure you have the right access to create that resource, and then it talks to a resource provider
for the service you·re creating. )or example, if you·re creating a new web app in Azure App 6er-
vice, ARM will pass your request on to the 
Microsoft.Web resource provider because it knows 
all about web apps and how to create them.
EXAM TIP
All management tools for Azure use ARM on the back end. That includes the Azure portal, 
Azure PowerShell, and the Azure CLI.
Management tools interact with ARM using the ARM application programming interface 
(API). The ARM API is the same whether you’re using the portal or command-line tools, which 
means you get a consistent result. It also means that you can create an Azure resource with the 
portal and then maNe changes to it using commandline tools, allowing you the Áexibility that 
cloud consumers need.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

MORE INFO VISUAL STUDIO AND ARM
Visual Studio, Microsoft’s development environment for developing applications, also can 
create Azure resources and deploy code to them. It does this using the same ARM API we’ve 
mentioned previously. In fact, you can think of the ARM API as your interface into the world 
of Azure. You really can’t create or manage any Azure services without going through the 
ARM API.
7he Áow of a typical ARM reTuest to create or manage a resource is straightforward. 7ools 
such as the Azure portal, commandline tools, or 9isual 6tudio maNe a reTuest to the ARM A3,. 
The API passes that request to ARM, where the user is authenticated and authorized to per-
form the action. ARM then passes the request to a resource provider, and the resource provider 
creates the new resource or modifies an existing resource. )igure 353 illustrates this Áow and 
features a small sampling of the many Azure services that are available.
FIGURE 3-53 Azure Resource Manager

 VISUAL STUDIO AND ARM

Skill 3.3: Describe features and tools for managing and deploying Azure resources CHAPTER 3 181
The request that is made to ARM isn’t a complicated, code-based request. Instead, ARM 
uses declarative syntax. That means as a consumer of Azure, you tell ARM what you want to do, 
and ARM does it for you. You don’t have to tell ARM how to do what you want. You simply tell 
it what you want. 7o do that, ARM uses files that are encoded in -ava6cript 2bMect 1otation or 
JSON) called ARM templates .
In the most basic sense, an ARM template contains a list of resources you want to create 
or modify. Each resource is accompanied by properties such as the name of the resource and 
properties that are specific to that resource. )or example, if you were using an ARM template to 
deploy a web app in App Service, your ARM template would specify the region you want your 
app to be created in, the name of the app, the pricing plan for your app, any domain names 
you want your app to use, and so forth. You don’t have to know how to set all those properties. 
You simply tell ARM to do it (you declare your intent to ARM), and ARM takes care of it for you.
MORE INFO MORE ON ARM TEMPLATES
ARM templates are incredibly powerful, but they’re also simple. If you want to 
read more about how to use ARM templates, check out the documentation at 
https://bit.ly/az900-armtemplates .
7here·s one more important aspect to ARM template deployment. :hen you·re deploying 
multiple resources (which, as pointed out, is a typical real-world scenario), you often have ser-
vice dependencies. In other words, you are deploying one or more services that rely on other 
services already being created.
)or example, thinN of a situation where you·re deploying a certificate to be used with a web 
app. 2ne of the properties you need to set on the web app is the certificate that you want to 
use, but if that certificate hasn·t been deployed yet, your deployment will fail. ARM allows you 
to specify dependencies so you can avoid issues like this. You simply tell ARM that the web 
app depends on the certificate, and ARM will ensure the certificate·s deployment is completed 
before it deploys the web app.
MORE INFO
 MORE ON ARM TEMPLATES
ARM templates are incredibly powerful, but they’re also simple. If you want to 
read
 more
 about how to use ARM templates, check out the documentation at 
https://bit.ly/az900-armtemplates

Thought experiment
/et·s wrap up this chapter with a thought experiment to help test your Nnowledge of the mate-
rial we covered. 7he answers to this thought experiment are in the section that follows.
Forecasting expenses
6o far, your record with &ontoso Medical *roup is positive. <ou·ve convinced the company that 
they need to move some of their deployments to Azure. As they plan that migration, the IT 
director needs to forecast the expenses for moving to the cloud. 7he ,7 director has asNed you 
to provide an estimate of cloud expenses based on the company·s current plan. :hat tools can 
you use to provide the estimate to the IT director? 
Categorizing expenses
7he ,7 director has also been speaNing to the &M* accounting office, and they·ve explained 
that they need an accurate breaNdown of cloud expenses from each internal organization at 
&M* to accurately report to the &)2 each month. :hat is your recommendation to provide 
this Nind of breaNdown of expenses"
Applying governance to resources
The IT director has been partnering with one of the application architects, and they’ve planned 
out the entire deployment. The architect has informed the IT director that as the number of us-
ers grows, they’ll need to add additional resources to keep performance high. Those resources 
include 9Ms, some ARM templates, and other Azure resources that must be configured in a 
specific way. 7he architect wants to ensure that when new resources are deployed, they are 
configured according to her specifications. 
:hat tools would you recommend to the architect to achieve these results"
Preventing the deletion of resources
The application also relies on a database that will be running in Azure. The database admin-
istrator needs some users to have elevated access to the database configuration in the Azure 
portal, but they are concerned that doing so runs the risk of someone accidentally deleting the 
database. They realize that the risk is low, but the result of that would be devastating, so they 
must account for that. 
How would you ease the mind of the database administrator and ensure that no one can 
delete the database?

7hought experiment answers CHAPTER 3 201
Effective deployment of Azure resources
One of the developers of the application has written an ARM template for deploying his part 
of the app. However, they need to deploy more than a dozen instances of the deployment 
defined in his template. ,t taNes a while to deploy the resources from the Azure portal, and the 
developer is interested in any option they might have for automating the deployment. The de-
veloper tells you that they have experience with many different scripting languages, but they·re

Monitoring application performance

Forecasting expenses

that and share it with the IT director.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

202 CHAPTER 3 Describe Azure management and governance
The total cost of ownership (TCO) calculator can help show you how much you’ll save by 
moving to Azure. One thing that it can show that might interest the IT director is how much 
you’ll save in areas like IT staff and infrastructure. This might help offset any concerns raised by 
the cost of Azure resources, especially because it can show you how much money you’ll save 
over time. 
Azure Cost Management and Billing may also help to alleviate any concerns because you 
can use it to easily keep an eye on costs and to create budgets so you can be alerted if your 
costs start to reach a configured threshold.
Categorizing expenses
7he accounting office has said they need a way to have an accurate breaNdown of expenses 
from each internal organization at &M*. By using tags on resources and tagging them with the 
organization·s name, the accounting office can easily meet this reTuirement. Because tags are 
shown on the Azure invoice, they are an excellent way to categorize cloud expenses.
Applying governance to resources
The IT director and architects want to ensure that new resources are deployed according to the 
exact specifications. ARM templates can certainly help with reliable and predictable deploy -
ments, and by using Azure 3olicy, you can ensure that deployments and their configuration 
meet company requirements. Also, you might want to recommend that they package their 
policies and templates into Azure Blueprints. 7his will allow them to repeat the exact deploy -
ments with policies attached as the application grows.
Preventing the deletion of resources
The database administrator must ensure that no one can delete the database. You might rec-
ommend RBAC to control this, but the admin has also said they need to give users a high level 
of access. To avoid problems with role assignments, you decide to recommend a delete lock on 
the database resource. This will allow the access levels the database administrator needs while 
ensuring that no one can delete the database. 
Effective deployment of Azure resources
One of the developers needs to automate the deployment of many resources, and they’d like a 
solution that can be used on an iPad as well as a computer. You recommend that the developer 
use either the PowerShell 
Az module or the Azure CLI. Both can be scripted, allowing for easy 
deployment of his resources. Because both PowerShell and the CLI are available in Azure Cloud 
Shell, the developer can use the Azure mobile app on an iPad to run either. Because Azure 
Cloud Shell can store scripts in Azure Storage, they’ll be available to the developer regardless 
of whether they’re using a computer or mobile device.
Humble Bundle Pearson Microsoft Press Exam Ref – © Pearson. Do Not Distribute.

Chapter summary CHAPTER 3 203
Monitoring application performance
The web administrator needs to keep an eye on the performance metrics of the app. You 
recommend that they enable Application Insights for the app. Doing so will also allow the web 
admin to create alerts in Azure Monitor so that they can automatically alert the right people if 
the metrics they are monitoring fall outside of configured values.
Reporting on the health of resources
Finally, the IT director wants to make sure that he can report on any application problem that is 
a result of an Azure incident. Azure 6ervice Health is the perfect solution for this. 1ot only does 
it give them the reporting they need, but they can download a PDF of any incident to share 
with the CTO during their business reviews.
Chapter summary
This chapter covered many topics related to costs, governance, management, and reporting.
